\mathsf{ST}

\mathsf{A}

V_{L}

V_{L}

V_{R}

V_{R}

V_{L}

V_{L}

\mathsf{B}

\mathsf{C_{1}}

\mathsf{C_{2}}

\vdots

\mathsf{C_{n}}

Notice that in the above we skip the intermediate labels on the
\mathsf{AB}
, this is because these are again maximally entangeled pairs completely existing in the database register. Finally, analyzing the database state from the Ideal experiment above and the real experiment from before we can define the simulator isometry (we call this isometry
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{comp}}
). To see how this is formally defined, we refer the reader to
Section

7.2
.

2.4.1
Bounding “Progress Measure”

The main challenge in demonstrating that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{comp}}
approximately maps the state in the real case close to the one in the ideal case is the difficulty of obtaining a simple closed-form expression, as was possible in the inverseless setting (see
[
MH24
]
, Appendix C). Instead, we draw inspiration from the query-by-query analysis approach in the literature of the quantum random oracle model
[
Zha19
,
DFMS22
]
. Specifically, we do query-by-query analysis via defining the
progress measure
as the adversary’s distinguishing advantage after each query.

A key step in our analysis is to show that, for any state
\lvert\psi\rangle
(generated using the real oracles), the process of first simulating the ideal database and then making a query to a ideal oracle (e.g.,
V^{\mathsf{fwd}}
) is close to making a query to a corresponding real oracle (e.g.,
V^{3,\mathsf{fwd}}V^{2,\mathsf{fwd}}V^{1,\mathsf{fwd}}
) first and then simulating the database. Formally, we show that the following two states are close:

V\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{comp}}\lvert\psi\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{comp}}V^{\mathsf{glued}}\lvert\psi\rangle\,,

which we establish by proving that the operator norm bound

\|(V\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{comp}}-\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{comp}}V^{\mathsf{glued}})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\mathsf{negl}(n)\,,

where
\Pi_{\leq t}
denotes the projector acting on the database register that checks that the database is of
\mathsf{poly}
-size.
Similarly, we extend this argument to show inverse queries too, i.e.

\|(V^{\dagger}\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{comp}}-\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{comp}}V^{\mathsf{glued},\dagger})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\mathsf{negl}(n)

To show this, we first show this in each of the subspaces and combine them to get the final result. Details for this can be found in
Section

7.4
. By establishing these bounds, we can inductively analyze the adversary’s distinguishing advantage after each query (for details, see
Section

7.3
). Hence, we show that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathsf{comp}}
approximately maps the state in the real case to the one in the ideal case.

3
Preliminaries

We denote the security parameter by
{\lambda}
. We assume that the reader is familiar with fundamentals of quantum computing, otherwise readers can refer to
[
NC10
]
. We refer to
\mathsf{negl}(\cdot)
to be a negligible function.

3.1
Notation

Indexing and sets

We use the notation
[n]
to refer to the set
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. For a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{n+m}
, let
x_{[1:n]}
to denote the first
n
bits of
x
. For
N,\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
N^{\downarrow\ell}=\prod_{i=0}^{\ell-1}(N-i)
.

Sets and set operators

For two binary strings of the same length
a,b
, we define
a\oplus b
to be the xor of the two strings. For a set of binary strings
A
and a binary string
b
, we define the set
A\oplus b:=\{a\oplus b\mid a\in A\}
. For two sets of same length binary strings
A
and
B
, we define the set
A\oplus B:=\{a\oplus b\mid a\in A,b\in B\}
.

Set products and the symmetric group

We use
\mathsf{Sym}_{t}
to refer to the symmetric group over
t
elements (i.e. the group of all permutations of
t
elements). Given a set
A
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
, we use the notation
A^{t}
to denote the
t
-fold Cartesian product of
A
, and the notation
A^{t}_{\mathrm{dist}}
to denote distinct subspace of
A^{t}
, i.e. the vectors in
A^{t}
,
\vec{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{t})
, such that for all
i\neq j
,
y_{i}\neq y_{j}
. We also define the set
\{\vec{x}\}:=\bigcup_{i\in[t]}\{x_{i}\}
.

Quantum states and distances

A register
\mathsf{R}
is a named finite-dimensional Hilbert space. If
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
are registers, then
\mathsf{AB}
denotes the tensor product of the two associated Hilbert spaces. We denote by
\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{R})
the density matrices over register
\mathsf{R}
. For
\rho_{\mathsf{AB}}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{AB})
, we let
\Tr_{\mathsf{B}}(\rho_{\mathsf{AB}})\in\mathcal{D}(\mathsf{A})
denote the reduced density matrix that results from taking the partial trace over
\mathsf{B}
. We denote by
\mathsf{TD}(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\norm{\rho- \rho'}_{1}
the trace distance between
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
, where
\norm{X}_{1}=\Tr(\sqrt{X^{\dagger} X})
is the trace norm. For two pure (and possibly subnormalized) states
\lvert\psi\rangle
and
\lvert\phi\rangle
, we use
\mathsf{TD}\quantity(\ket{\psi},\ket{\phi})
as a shorthand for
\mathsf{TD}\quantity(\proj{\psi},\proj{\phi})
. We also say that
A\preceq B
if
B-A
is a positive semi-definite matrix. For positive integers
t,d\in\mathbb{N}
and a permutation
\sigma\in\mathsf{Sym}_{t}
, we let
P_{d}(\sigma)
be the
d^{t}
-dimensional unitary that acts on registers
\mathsf{R}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{R}_{t}
by permuting the registers according to
\sigma
. That is,

P_{d}(\sigma)\lvert x_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{R}_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\lvert x_{t}\rangle_{\mathsf{R}_{t}}:=\lvert x_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\rangle_{\mathsf{R}_{1}}\otimes\dots\otimes\lvert x_{\sigma^{-1}(t)}\rangle_{\mathsf{R}_{t}}

for all
(x_{1},\dots,x_{t})\in[d]^{t}
. We denote by
\mathcal{H}_{n}
the Haar distribution over
n
-qubit states, and
\mu_{n}
the Haar measure over
n
-qubit unitaries (i.e. the unique left and right invariant measure).

Relations

Relations are an important part of the path recording framework, here we define relations between sets, as well as what it means to be injective and to take the inverse of a relation.

Definition 3.1

(Relation)
.

A relation between two finite sets
X
and
Y
is a
multiset
of tuples
\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i\in[t]}
with
x_{i}\in X
and
y_{i}\in Y
for all
i\in[t]
.

Definition 3.2

(
\operatorname{Dom}(R)
and
\imaginary(R)
)
.

For a relation
R=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{t}
, define
\operatorname{Dom}(R)=\{x_{i}\}_{i\in[t]}
and
\imaginary(R)=\{y_{i}\}_{i\in[t]}
.

Definition 3.3

(Inverse of a relation)
.

The inverse of a relation
R=\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{t}
is the relation from
Y
to
X
defined by
R^{-1}=\{(y_{i},x_{i})\}_{i=1}^{t}

Definition 3.4

(Substrings)
.

Given a string
x\in\{0,1\}^{2n+{\lambda}}
, let
x^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}
,
x^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}
, and
x^{\mathfrak{r}(n)}
represent the sub-string on the first
n
, middle
\lambda
, and final
n
bits respectively, so that
x=x^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}||x^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}||x^{\mathfrak{r}(n)}
. Also define
\mathfrak{l}(\cdot)
,
\mathfrak{r}(\cdot)
and
\mathfrak{m}(\cdot)
for vectors and sets of strings as follows, let
S=\{x_{i}\}_{i\in[t]}
, then
S^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}=\{x^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}_{i}\}_{i\in[t]}
and let
\overrightarrow{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{t})
, then
\overrightarrow{x^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}}=(x^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}_{1},\ldots,x^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}_{t})
.
2
2

2

Let
\mathfrak{l}(\cdot)
,
\mathfrak{r}(\cdot)
and
\mathfrak{m}(\cdot)
can be defined on strings of other lengths too as first, last and middle substring of some length.

3.2
Cryptographic Primitives

In this section, we define strong pseudorandom unitaries (strong PRU)
[
JLS18
]
, which are the quantum equivalent of a pseudorandom function, in that an adversary can not distinguish the strong PRU from a truly Haar random unitary, even with inverse access to both.

Definition 3.5

(Strong pseudorandom unitaries)
.

We say that a quantum polynomial-size circuit
G
is a strong pseudorandom unitary if for all quantum polynomial-time adversaries
\mathcal{A}
, there exists a negligible function
\epsilon
such that for all
{\lambda}
,

\left|\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{k\leftarrow\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}}\left[1\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{G_{\lambda}(k),{G_{\lambda}(k)}^{\dagger}}\right]-\mathop{\mathrm{Pr}}_{\mathcal{U}\leftarrow\mu_{n({\lambda})}}\left[1\leftarrow\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}^{\mathcal{U},\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}}\right]\right|\leq\epsilon({\lambda})\,.

In the
\mathsf{QHROM}
, both
G_{\lambda}
and
\mathcal{A}_{{\lambda}}
have oracle access to an additional family of unitaries
\{U_{{\lambda}}\}_{{\lambda}\in\mathbb{N}}
sampled from the Haar measure on
{\lambda}
qubits, and their inverses.

3.3
Useful Lemmas

Here we present useful quantum lemmas that should be familiar to a reader well versed in quantum computation.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any operator
A
and vector
\lvert\psi\rangle
,
\norm{A \ket{\psi}}_{2}\leq\norm{A}_{\mathrm{op}}\norm{\ket{\psi}}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
A
be an operator and
{\mathcal{B}}
be an orthonormal basis of the domain of
A
. If
A\lvert i\rangle
is orthogonal to
A\lvert j\rangle
for all
\lvert i\rangle\neq\lvert j\rangle\in{\mathcal{B}}
, then
\norm{A}_{\mathrm{op}}=\max_{\lvert i\rangle\in{\mathcal{B}}}\norm{A \ket{i}}_{2}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
be two projectors, then
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
commute if and only if their product is a projector.

4
Path Recording and its Variants

In this section, we recall the path recording framework from
[
MH24
]
. We give a relatively more general way of looking at path recording and a general theorem for working with path recording. Finally, we define a variant of path recording that we will use throughout this paper.

4.1
Interpreting the Path Recording Framework

Here we recall the path recording framework from
[
MH24
]
. The path recording framework was defined as a purification of querying a Haar unitary.

We will start by stating and interpreting how to "simulate" just forward queries to a Haar unitary. Define the following operator: for any injective relations
R
,

\mathsf{PR}:\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{R}}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|R|}}\sum_{y\notin\imaginary(R)}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert R\cup\{(x,y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{R}}.

[
MH24
]
gives the following theorem that, in essence, shows that
\mathsf{PR}
simulates forward queries to a Haar unitary.

Theorem 4.1

(
[
MH24
, Theorem 5]
)
.

For any
t
-query algorithm
{\cal A}=\left(A_{1},\ldots,A_{t}\right)
,

\mathsf{TD}\quantity( \Ex_{U \sim\haarunitaries_n}\ketbra{\Adversary_t^{U}}{\Adversary_t^{U}}, \Tr_{\reg{R}} \left( \ketbra{\Adversary_t^{\pr}}{\Adversary_t^{\pr}}\right) )\leq O\quantity(\frac{t^2}{N^{1/2}}),

where
N=2^{n},\lvert{\cal A}_{t}^{U}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(UA_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}
and
\lvert{\cal A}_{t}^{\mathsf{PR}}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(\mathsf{PR}A_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{R}}
.

To understand how
\mathsf{PR}
works, we must first understand what querying a Haar unitary looks like. In general, querying a Haar unitary on any state gives a maximally mixed state. To understand how
\mathsf{PR}
does this, we first notice that if you have a maximally entangled state
\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{x\in\{0,1\}^{n}}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}
on two registers
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
, then if you only have access to one of these registers
\mathsf{A}
or
\mathsf{B}
, the state looks maximally mixed.
\mathsf{PR}
uses this property to answer queries to a Haar unitary. In particular, on a query to
\mathsf{PR}
, it saves the input in the purification register and creates a maximally entangled pair, saves one half in the purification register (with the label as the input), and returns the other half as output on the query register.

The above idea works almost perfectly, except the operation we define above is not "reversible" (i.e. isn’t an isometry or physically realizable). To fix this, we define
\mathsf{PR}
as only creating an almost maximally entangled state and returns a half in the query register while saving the other in the purification register (labeled by the input) in a way that the operation is still "reversible".

Next
[
MH24
]
extends this idea to both forward and inverse queries to the Haar unitary. Define the following two operators: for any relations
L,R
,

V_{L}:\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|\imaginary(L\cup R^{-1})|}}\sum_{y\notin\imaginary(L\cup R^{-1})}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L\cup\{(x,y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{T}},

V_{R}:\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{N-|\imaginary(R\cup L^{-1})|}}\sum_{y\notin\imaginary(R\cup L^{-1})}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert L\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\cup\{(x,y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}.

Using
V_{L}
and
V_{R}
, they define the following partial isometry:

V=V_{L}\cdot(I-V_{R}\cdot V_{R}^{\dagger})+(I-V_{L}\cdot V_{L}^{\dagger})\cdot V_{R}^{\dagger}.

Theorem 4.2

(
[
MH24
, Theorem 8]
)
.

For any
2t
-query algorithm
{\cal A}=\left(A_{1},B_{1},\ldots,A_{t},B_{t}\right)
,

\mathsf{TD}\quantity( \Ex_{U \sim\haarunitaries_n}\ketbra{\Adversary_t^{U,U^{\dagger}}}{\Adversary_t^{U,U^{\dagger}}}, \Tr_{\reg{ST}} \left( \ketbra{\Adversary_t^{V,V^{\dagger}}}{\Adversary_t^{V,V^{\dagger}}}\right) )\leq O\quantity(\frac{t^2}{N^{1/8}}),

where
N=2^{n},\lvert{\cal A}_{t}^{U,U^{\dagger}}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(U^{\dagger}B_{i}UA_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}
and
\lvert{\cal A}_{t}^{V,V^{\dagger}}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(V^{\dagger}B_{i}VA_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}
.

To understand how
V
works, we use the following intuition: given access to the Haar unitary and its inverse, this almost looks like two independent Haar unitaries except if the inverse is queried on the output of a forward query, one must reverse the forward query. To do this, we can think of instantiating two independent forward query oracles
V_{L}
and
V_{R}
, except whenever the inverse oracle is queried, first we check if the input is entangled with something in the purification of
V_{L}
(i.e. was the output of
V_{L}
), if it is, return the associated label, else, just act as a forward query to
V_{R}
. Again, to make the above operator "reversible" (i.e. an isometry), we restrict how the "almost" maximally entangled state is defined.

Another way of looking at the above operation
V
that maps well to our intuition is the following: First define the following two projectors (as the projectors that check entanglement between the query register and the database register corresponding to
V_{L}
(and
V_{R}
, respectively)):

\displaystyle\Pi^{L}

\displaystyle=V_{L}\cdot V_{L}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\Pi^{R}

\displaystyle=V_{R}\cdot V_{R}^{\dagger}

Then we can see that
V
can be written as

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\Pi^{L}\cdot V_{L}\cdot(I-\Pi^{R})+(I-\Pi^{L})\cdot V_{R}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{R}

\displaystyle V^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\Pi^{R}\cdot V_{R}\cdot(I-\Pi^{L})+(I-\Pi^{R})\cdot V_{L}^{\dagger}\cdot\Pi^{L}

Hence operationally, we interpret
V
as two branches:

•

If the query register is maximally entangled with the database of
V_{R}
, apply
V_{R}^{\dagger}
,

•

Else apply
V_{L}
.

In the above, we can think of first checking what subspace a query lies in and then applying the operation depending on this subspace.

4.2
A Generalization of the Path-Recording Framework

In this section, we will define a generalization of the Path-Recording Framework. The main intuition behind this generalization is that when applying
V_{L}
or
V_{R}
, one doesn’t necessarily need a maximally entangled state, just a state with "enough" entanglement. Consider the following operation: Let
V_{L}
acts on some query register
\mathsf{A}
and some purification register
\mathsf{ST}
, and added an entry in the database saved in the register
\mathsf{S}
. Then an operation
V^{f}_{L}
action on some query register
\mathsf{A}
and some purification register
\mathsf{ST}
and an ancilla register
\mathsf{B}
, is close to
V_{L}
where
f
acts on
\mathsf{ABST}
and outputs a "large enough" subset of
[N]\setminus\imaginary(S)
over which we create the highly entangled state.

Formally, we define the following two operators:

\displaystyle V^{f_{L}}_{L}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert z\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|f_{L}(x,z,L,R)|}}\sum_{y\in f_{L}(x,z,L,R)}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert z\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L\cup\{(x,y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle V^{f_{R}}_{R}\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert z\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{|f_{R}(x,z,L,R)|}}\sum_{y\in f_{R}(x,z,L,R)}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert z\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert L\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\cup\{(x,y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}

where for all
x,z,L,R
,
f_{L}(x,z,L,R)\subseteq[N]\setminus\imaginary(L)
and
f_{R}(x,z,L,R)\subseteq[N]\setminus\imaginary(R)
.

Using above, we can define
V^{f_{L},f_{R}}
as

V^{f_{L},f_{R}}=V^{f_{L}}_{L}\cdot(I-V^{f_{R}}_{R}\cdot V^{f_{R},\dagger}_{R})+(I-V^{f_{L}}_{L}\cdot V^{f_{L},\dagger}_{L})\cdot V^{f_{R},\dagger}_{R}.

Then we can show that as long as
f_{L}
and
f_{R}
give large enough subsets, to a poly query algorithm,
V
and
V^{f_{L},f_{R}}
are indistinguishable. Formally, we have the following:

Lemma 4.3

(Generalized Path-Recording)
.

Let
f_{L}
and
f_{R}
be functions such that for all
x,z,L,R
,
|L|+|R|\leq t
,

\displaystyle f_{L}(x,z,L,R)

\displaystyle\subseteq[N]\setminus\imaginary(L)

\displaystyle f_{R}(x,z,L,R)

\displaystyle\subseteq[N]\setminus\imaginary(R)

\displaystyle\frac{\left|N-|f_{L}(x,z,L,R)|-t\right|}{|f_{L}(x,z,L,R)|}

\displaystyle\leq\delta

\displaystyle\frac{\left|N-|f_{R}(x,z,L,R)|-t\right|}{|f_{R}(x,z,L,R)|}

\displaystyle\leq\delta,

For any
2t
-query algorithm
{\cal A}=\left(A_{1},B_{1},\ldots,A_{t},B_{t}\right)
,

\mathsf{TD}\quantity( \Tr_{\reg{ST}} \left( \ketbra{\Adversary_t^{V^{f_L,f_R},V^{f_L,f_R,\dagger}}}{\Adversary_t^{V^{f_L,f_R},V^{f_L,f_R,\dagger}}}\right), \Tr_{\reg{ST}} \left( \ketbra{\Adversary_t^{V,V^{\dagger}}}{\Adversary_t^{V,V^{\dagger}}}\right) )\leq 16t\sqrt{2\cdot(t+1)\cdot\delta},

where,

\displaystyle\lvert{\cal A}_{t}^{V^{f_{L},f_{R}},V^{f_{L},f_{R},\dagger}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(V^{f_{L},f_{R},\dagger}B_{i}V^{f_{L},f_{R}}A_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}

\displaystyle\lvert{\cal A}_{t}^{V,V^{\dagger}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(V^{\dagger}B_{i}VA_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{B}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}

The proof of the above lemma is provided in
Appendix

A
.

4.3
Modified Path Recording

In this subsection, we define a specific restriction on path recording that we will use later in the proof. We define the following restricted path recording operator as:

W^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}_{L}:\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{2n}(2^{{\lambda}}-|\imaginary(L\cup R)^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}|)}}\sum_{y:y^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}\notin\imaginary(L\cup R)^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L\cup\{(x,y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{T}},

W^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}_{R}:\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{2n}(2^{{\lambda}}-|\imaginary(L\cup R)^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}|)}}\sum_{y:y^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}\notin\imaginary(L\cup R)^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert R\cup\{(x,y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}.

Finally, define:

W^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}=W_{L}^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}\cdot(I-W_{R}^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}\cdot W_{R}^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda}),\dagger})+(I-W_{L}^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}\cdot W_{L}^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda}),\dagger})\cdot W_{R}^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda}),\dagger}

Then from
Lemma

4.3
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
2t
-query algorithm
{\cal A}=\left(A_{1},B_{1},\ldots,A_{t},B_{t}\right)
,

\mathsf{TD}\quantity( \Tr_{\reg{ST}} \left( \ketbra{\Adversary_t^{W^{\frm(\secp)},W^{\frm(\secp),\dagger}}}{\Adversary_t^{W^{\frm(\secp)},W^{\frm(\secp),\dagger}}}\right), \Tr_{\reg{ST}} \left( \ketbra{\Adversary_t^{V,V^{\dagger}}}{\Adversary_t^{V,V^{\dagger}}}\right) )\leq O\quantity(\sqrt{\frac{t^{3}}{2^{\secp}}}),

where
|\mathsf{ABC}|=2n+{\lambda}
,
\lvert{\cal A}_{t}^{W^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})},W^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda}),\dagger}}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(W^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda}),\dagger}B_{i}W^{\mathfrak{m}({\lambda})}A_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{D}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}
and
\lvert{\cal A}_{t}^{V,V^{\dagger}}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(V^{\dagger}B_{i}VA_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{D}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T}}
.

5
Glued Haar Unitary and its Purification

In this section, we present our glued Haar random unitary construction. We study an operation that acts on a purification register and mimics as if querying a glued Haar random unitary construction (similar to how path recording mimics a single Haar unitary). We’ll call this operator the Glued Path Recording operator. Similar to how Path Recording has different actions on two different subspaces, Glued Path Recording has different actions on four different subspaces.

We start by looking at the naive way to achieve this by just replacing each Haar unitary in the glued construction with an instance of Path Recording Instance. Next, we will define a modification of the Path Recording oracle that is easier to work with in the proof. Finally, we define the four subspaces of interest and, using these subspaces, we define an operator that acts as the Glued Path Recording.

5.1
Glued Haar Unitary Construction

In this section, we present our construction of the Glued Haar Unitary. Let
U^{1},U^{2}
and
U^{3}
be unitaries acting on
n+{\lambda}
qubits, then we define the glued construction
G(U^{1},U^{2},U^{3})
acting on
2n+{\lambda}
qubits as:

G(U^{1},U^{2},U^{3})_{\mathsf{ABC}}=U_{\mathsf{AB}}^{3}\cdot U_{\mathsf{BC}}^{2}\cdot U_{\mathsf{AB}}^{1}

where
|\mathsf{A}|=|\mathsf{C}|=n
,
|\mathsf{B}|={\lambda}
.

Throughout this paper, we show that an if
U_{1},U_{2}
and
U_{3}
were sampled from the Haar distribution, then no poly query algorithm can distinguish between
G(U^{1},U^{2},U^{3})
and its inverse from a Haar random untary
O
and its inverse (where
O
acts on
2n+{\lambda}
qubits).

We start by studying a purification of the construction
G(U^{1},U^{2},U^{3})
. Notice that one can always just purify
U^{1}
,
U^{2}
, and
U^{3}
individually using Path Recording. While this is a valid purification, it seems hard to work with. We give a more intuitive purification of the above construction in the next section.

5.2
Glued Path Recording

To get a more intuitive purification of the glued Haar unitary construction, we first look at how the construction behaves. Let we make a query to the construction, then we know, from the discussion in
Section

4.1
, that if this query is a previous output of
U^{1}
(which in the purified view looks like checking entanglement), then
U^{1}
inverts this query; else
U^{1}
returns an extremely scrambled state.

Notice that the output of
U^{1}
is (partially) fed into
U^{2}
.
U^{2}
performs a similar check to see if the query is a previous output of
U^{2}
or not. Interestingly, notice that if
U^{1}
was returning an extremely scrambled state, then this state would almost certainly not be the output of a previous query to
U^{2}
.

To look at this in the purified view, if
U^{1}
created a new maximally entangled pair and returns one half in the query register and saves the other half in the first database register, then when
U^{2}
checks if this state is maximally entangled with something in the second database, this check almost always fails because by monogamy of entanglement we know that if the query register is maximally entangled with something in the first database, it cannot be maximally entangled with anything in the second database.

To formalise this intuition, we start defining some operations similar to path recording. In particular, corresponding to
U^{1}
(and
U^{2}
and
U^{3}
, respectively), we define a pair of operations
(V^{1}_{L},V^{1}_{R})
(and
(V^{2}_{L},V^{2}_{R})
and
(V^{3}_{L},V^{3}_{R})
, respectively) where
(V^{1}_{L},V^{1}_{R})
(and
(V^{2}_{L},V^{2}_{R})
and
(V^{3}_{L},V^{3}_{R})
, respectively) have corresponding purification registers
\mathsf{S_{1}T_{1}}
(and
\mathsf{S_{2}T_{2}}
and
\mathsf{S_{3}T_{3}}
, respectively).

Now we define some projectors that correspond to checking entanglements (similar to
\Pi^{R}
in
Section

4.1
):

\displaystyle\Pi^{R,1}

\displaystyle=V_{R}^{1}V_{R}^{1,\dagger}

\displaystyle\Pi^{R,12}

\displaystyle=V_{R}^{1}V_{R}^{2}V_{R}^{2,\dagger}V_{R}^{1,\dagger}

\displaystyle\Pi^{R,123}

\displaystyle=V_{R}^{1}V_{R}^{2}V_{R}^{3}V_{R}^{3,\dagger}V_{R}^{2,\dagger}V_{R}^{1,\dagger}

Similarly, we define similar projectors in the opposite direction (similar to
\Pi^{L}
in
Section

4.1
):

\displaystyle\Pi^{L,3}

\displaystyle=V_{L}^{3}V_{L}^{3,\dagger}

\displaystyle\Pi^{L,32}

\displaystyle=V_{L}^{3}V_{L}^{2}V_{L}^{2,\dagger}V_{L}^{3,\dagger}

\displaystyle\Pi^{L,321}

\displaystyle=V_{L}^{3}V_{L}^{2}V_{L}^{1}V_{L}^{1,\dagger}V_{L}^{2,\dagger}V_{L}^{3,\dagger}

Then with these in mind, we define the glued purification as follows:

\displaystyle V^{\mathsf{glued}}=

\displaystyle\left(\Pi^{L,321}\right)\cdot V_{L}^{3}\cdot V_{L}^{2}\cdot V_{L}^{1}\cdot\left(I-\Pi^{R,1}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\Pi^{L,32}-\Pi^{L,321}\right)\cdot V_{L}^{3}\cdot V_{L}^{2}\cdot V_{R}^{1,\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{R,1}-\Pi^{R,12}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\Pi^{L,3}-\Pi^{L,32}\right)\cdot V_{L}^{3}\cdot V_{R}^{2,\dagger}\cdot V_{R}^{1,\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{R,12}-\Pi^{R,123}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(I-\Pi^{L,3}\right)\cdot V_{R}^{3,\dagger}\cdot V_{R}^{2,\dagger}\cdot V_{R}^{1,\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{R,123}\right)

Operationally, the
V^{\mathsf{glued}}
works as follows:

•

Check if the query register is in the output of
V_{R}^{1}\cdot V_{R}^{2}\cdot V_{R}^{3}
, if it is, invert these queries.

•

Else, check if the query register is in the output of
V_{R}^{1}\cdot V_{R}^{2}
, if it is, invert these queries and apply
V_{L}^{3}
.

•

Else, check if the query register is in the output of
V_{R}^{1}
, if it is, invert this query and apply
V_{L}^{3}\cdot V_{L}^{2}
.

•

Else, apply
V_{L}^{3}\cdot V_{L}^{2}\cdot V_{L}^{1}
.

One can see that
V^{\mathsf{glued}}
has a structure that is similar to path recording while also following the intuition stated at the beginning. In fact using the intuition from the discussion above, we formally show the following:

Lemma 5.1
.

For any
2t
-query algorithm
{\cal A}=\left(A_{1},B_{1},\ldots,A_{t},B_{t}\right)
,

\norm{\ket{\Adversary^{V^{\glued}, V^{\glued,\dagger}}}_{\reg{ABCD\overline{ST}}}
- \ket{\Adversary^{V^{3}V^{2}V^{1}, (V^{3}V^{2}V^{1})^{\dagger}}}_{\reg{ABCD\overline{ST}}}}_{2}=O\quantity(\frac{t^3}{2^{\secp}}).

where
\mathsf{\overline{ST}}=\mathsf{S_{1}S_{2}S_{3}T_{1}T_{2}T_{3}}
,
|\mathsf{ABC}|=2n+{\lambda}
,

\lvert{\cal A}_{t}^{V^{\mathsf{glued}},V^{\mathsf{glued},\dagger}}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(V^{\mathsf{glued},\dagger}B_{i}V^{\mathsf{glued}}A_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{D}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S_{1}}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T_{1}}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S_{2}}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T_{2}}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{S_{3}}}\lvert\varnothing\rangle_{\mathsf{T_{3}}}

and
\lvert{\cal A}^{V^{3}V^{2}V^{1},(V^{3}V^{2}V^{1})^{\dagger}}\rangle_{\mathsf{ABCD\overline{ST}}}
is defined similarly.

The proof of the above lemma is provided in
Appendix

B
.

5.3
Modified Glued Path Recording

In this subsection, we define a specific restriction on the glued path recording that we will use later in the proof. We define the following restricted glued path recording operator as:

We first define the following

\imaginary_{1}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})=\imaginary(L_{1}\cup R_{3})^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}\bigcup\imaginary(L_{2}\cup R_{2})^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}\bigcup\operatorname{Dom}(L_{2}\cup R_{2})^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})},

and

\imaginary_{2}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})=\imaginary(L_{3}\cup R_{1})^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}.

Next, we define the following partial isometries:

\displaystyle V^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}_{L}

\displaystyle\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}(2^{{\lambda}}-|\imaginary_{1}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})|)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y:\\
y^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}\notin\imaginary_{1}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\end{subarray}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{AB}}\lvert x^{\mathfrak{r}(n)}\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle\otimes\lvert L_{1}\cup\{(x^{\mathfrak{l}(n+{\lambda})},y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle V^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}

\displaystyle\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}(2^{{\lambda}}-|\imaginary_{2}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})|)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y:\\
y^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}\notin\imaginary_{2}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\end{subarray}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{AB}}\lvert x^{\mathfrak{r}(n)}\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle\otimes\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\cup\{(x^{\mathfrak{l}(n+{\lambda})},y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle V^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}_{L}

\displaystyle\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}(2^{{\lambda}}-|\imaginary_{1}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})|)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y:\\
y^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}\notin\imaginary_{1}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\end{subarray}}\lvert x^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{BC}}

\displaystyle\otimes\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\cup\{(x^{\mathfrak{r}(n+{\lambda})},y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle V^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}

\displaystyle\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}(2^{{\lambda}}-|\imaginary_{1}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})|)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y:\\
y^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}\notin\imaginary_{1}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\end{subarray}}\lvert x^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}\rangle_{\mathsf{A}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{BC}}

\displaystyle\otimes\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\cup\{(x^{\mathfrak{r}(n+{\lambda})},y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle V^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}_{L}

\displaystyle\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}(2^{{\lambda}}-|\imaginary_{2}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})|)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y:\\
y^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}\notin\imaginary_{2}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\end{subarray}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{AB}}\lvert x^{\mathfrak{r}(n)}\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle\otimes\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\cup\{(x^{\mathfrak{l}(n+{\lambda})},y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle V^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}

\displaystyle\lvert x\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}(2^{{\lambda}}-|\imaginary_{1}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})|)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y:\\
y^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}\notin\imaginary_{1}^{\mathsf{mid}}(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})\end{subarray}}\lvert y\rangle_{\mathsf{AB}}\lvert x^{\mathfrak{r}(n)}\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle\otimes\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{3}\cup\{(x^{\mathfrak{l}(n+{\lambda})},y)\}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}

We know by
Lemma

A.2
that for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
and
X\in\{L,R\}
,

\|(V^{i}_{X}-V^{(i),\mathsf{mid}}_{X})\Pi_{\leq t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O\left(\sqrt{\frac{t^{2}}{2^{\lambda}}}\right).

The reason the above operators are defined this way will become clear in the next section. Basically, querying glued path recording using the above operators results in a mbetter-structuredured purification. We next define the modified path recording with the above operator.

Now we define some projectors that correspond to checking entanglements (similar to
Section

5.2
):

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}

\displaystyle=V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}

\displaystyle=V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}V_{R}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}V_{R}^{(2),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}

\displaystyle=V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}V_{R}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}V_{R}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}V_{R}^{(3),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}V_{R}^{(2),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}

Similarly, we define similar projectors in the opposite direction (similar to
Section

5.2
):

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},3}

\displaystyle=V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},32}

\displaystyle=V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}V_{L}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}V_{L}^{(2),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}

\displaystyle\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},321}

\displaystyle=V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}V_{L}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}V_{L}^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}V_{L}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}V_{L}^{(2),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}

Then with these in mind, we define the glued purification as follows:

\displaystyle W^{\mathsf{glued}}=

\displaystyle\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},321}\right)\cdot V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{L}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{L}^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot\left(I-\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},32}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},321}\right)\cdot V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{L}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},3}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},32}\right)\cdot V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{R}^{(2),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(I-\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},3}\right)\cdot V_{R}^{(3),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot V_{R}^{(2),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}\right)

Then we have the following:

Lemma 5.2
.

For any adversary
{\cal A}
that makes
t
forward queries and
t
inverse queries,

\norm{\ket{\Adversary^{V^{\glued}, V^{\glued,\dagger}}}_{\reg{ABCD\overline{ST}}}
- \ket{\Adversary^{W^{\glued}, W^{\glued,\dagger}}}_{\reg{ABCD\overline{ST}}}}_{2}=O\quantity(\sqrt{\frac{t^4}{2^{\secp}}}),

where
\mathsf{\overline{ST}}=\mathsf{S_{1}S_{2}S_{3}T_{1}T_{2}T_{3}}
.

For ease of notation, we define the following projectors:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathfrak{l},1}

\displaystyle=I-\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathfrak{l},2}

\displaystyle=\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathfrak{l},3}

\displaystyle=\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathfrak{l},4}

\displaystyle=\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}

6
Structure of Purification for
W^{\mathsf{glued}}

In this section, our main goal would be to understand the structure of purification when using glued path recording. To do this, we start by showing how the purification can be associated to a graph, how this graph is structured and can be parametrized. We then use these graphs to define "good" states. Once we have defined the good states, we study intersection of good states and the subspaces defined with respect to glued path recording. Finally, we show that querying glued path recording only leads to good states.

The main intuition behind studying the structure is the following: Looking at the operational definition of
V^{\mathsf{glued}}
in
Section

5.2
, we can see that the queries can only be done in certain patterns. In particular, any output of
V^{2}_{L}
is partially fed as input to a
V^{3}_{L}
. Similarly, whenever we try to invert
V^{3}_{L}
(i.e. apply
V^{3,\dagger}_{L}
), it is followed by either inverting
V^{2}_{L}
(i.e. apply
V^{2,\dagger}_{L}
) or applying
V^{2}_{R}
. We associate a graph with the database to keep track of these correlations (i.e. keep track of when the output of some operator is part fed as the input to another operation).

6.1
Graph associated with the Database

To study the structure of the purification when querying
W^{\mathsf{glued}}
and
W^{\mathsf{glued},\dagger}
, we start by associating a graph to the database in the purification. To do this, we associate a vertex for each entry in the database. We label these vertices with the tuple in the database as well as what database it came from. Formally we do the following:
Given
(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
, we define the following vertex set:

\displaystyle V_{L_{1}}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathfrak{l}_{1},x,y)|(x,y)\in L_{1}\}

\displaystyle V_{L_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathfrak{l}_{2},x,y)|(x,y)\in L_{2}\}

\displaystyle V_{L_{3}}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathfrak{l}_{3},x,y)|(x,y)\in L_{3}\}

\displaystyle V_{R_{1}}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathfrak{r}_{1},x,y)|(x,y)\in R_{1}\}

\displaystyle V_{R_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathfrak{r}_{2},x,y)|(x,y)\in R_{2}\}

\displaystyle V_{R_{3}}

\displaystyle=\{(\mathfrak{r}_{3},x,y)|(x,y)\in R_{3}\}\,.

Next we want to add edges to the graph. We add edges where we suspect that the output of a given query was partially fed as input to another query. Looking back at the operational definition of
V^{\mathsf{glued}}
(in
Section

5.2
), we see that in some operations correlate various enties in this form. We define directed edges between such tuples as follows:

\displaystyle E_{L_{1}L_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{(v_{1},v_{2})|v_{1}=(\mathfrak{l}_{1},x_{1},y_{1})\in V_{L_{1}},v_{2}=(\mathfrak{l}_{2},x_{2},y_{2})\in V_{L_{2}},{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}={x_{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}\}

\displaystyle E_{L_{2}L_{3}}

\displaystyle=\{(v_{1},v_{2})|v_{1}=(\mathfrak{l}_{2},x_{1},y_{1})\in V_{L_{2}},v_{2}=(\mathfrak{l}_{3},x_{2},y_{2})\in V_{L_{3}},{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}={x_{2}}^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}\}

\displaystyle E_{R_{3}R_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{(v_{1},v_{2})|v_{1}=(\mathfrak{r}_{3},x_{1},y_{1})\in V_{R_{3}},v_{2}=(\mathfrak{r}_{2},x_{2},y_{2})\in V_{R_{2}},{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}={x_{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}\}

\displaystyle E_{R_{2}R_{1}}

\displaystyle=\{(v_{1},v_{2})|v_{1}=(\mathfrak{r}_{2},x_{1},y_{1})\in V_{R_{2}},v_{2}=(\mathfrak{r}_{1},x_{2},y_{2})\in V_{R_{1}},{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}={x_{2}}^{\mathfrak{r}({\lambda})}\}

\displaystyle E_{L_{2}R_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{(v_{1},v_{2})|v_{1}=(\mathfrak{l}_{2},x_{1},y_{1})\in V_{L_{2}},v_{2}=(\mathfrak{r}_{2},x_{2},y_{2})\in V_{R_{2}},{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}={x_{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}\}

\displaystyle E_{R_{2}L_{2}}

\displaystyle=\{(v_{1},v_{2})|v_{1}=(\mathfrak{r}_{2},x_{1},y_{1})\in V_{R_{2}},v_{2}=(\mathfrak{l}_{2},x_{2},y_{2})\in V_{L_{2}},{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}={x_{2}}^{\mathfrak{l}({\lambda})}\}\,.

Finally, we this gives us the graph:

\displaystyle V(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i=1}^{3}\left(V_{L_{i}}\cup V_{R_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle E(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i=1}^{2}\left(E_{L_{i}L_{i+1}}\cup E_{R_{i+1}R_{i}}\right)\cup E_{L_{2}R_{2}}\cup E_{R_{2}L_{2}}

\displaystyle G(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})

\displaystyle=(V(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}),E(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}))

6.2
Analyzing Structure of the Graph

Next we try to study the structure of this graph associated with the database. We know that we added an edge at every instance where we suspect that an output from some operator was partially fed into another operator as the input. Next, we will try to see "chains" of multiple operators where the output of one is fed as input to another. These would look like a path in the associated graph.

A path in the graph to a sequence of connected vertices
(v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{n})
with edges
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
. A graph is a line graph if all the vertices in the graph form a path and all edges in the graph are just part of the path. We say a graph is a linear forest if it is a disjoint union of line graphs. For any linear forest, let
{\mathcal{P}}(G)
be the set of disjoint line graphs. For any
p\in{\mathcal{P}}(G)
, let
\mathsf{len}(p)
denote the number of edges in
p
.

Looking back at the operational description of
V^{\mathsf{glued}}
, operations always start with either
V_{L}^{1}
or
V_{R}^{3}
. Similarly, operations always end with either
V_{L}^{3}
or
V_{R}^{1}
. Hence, we can classify the lines in the linear forest associated to the database. Formally, we define the following classes of Line Graphs in
G
.

Definition 6.1

(Classes of Line Graphs in
G
)
.

Given a collection of relations,
\vec{L}=(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}),\vec{R}=(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
, define the following sets:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{LL}(\vec{L},\vec{R})

\displaystyle=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}}(G(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}))|p_{\sf start}\in L_{1},p_{\sf end}\in L_{3}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{LR}(\vec{L},\vec{R})

\displaystyle=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}}(G(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}))|p_{\sf start}\in L_{1},p_{\sf end}\in R_{1}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{RL}(\vec{L},\vec{R})

\displaystyle=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}}(G(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}))|p_{\sf start}\in R_{3},p_{\sf end}\in L_{3}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{RR}(\vec{L},\vec{R})

\displaystyle=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}}(G(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}))|p_{\sf start}\in R_{3},p_{\sf end}\in R_{1}\}

Next, notice that while part of the output of
V^{1}_{L}
is fed into
V^{2}_{L}
, the other part of the output is fed to the last operator in the path. We refer the reader to figures in the
Section

2.3
. We formalise this to define good line in graphs:

Definition 6.2

(Good lines in
G
)
.

Given a collection of relations,
\vec{L}=(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3}),\vec{R}=(R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
, define the following subsets of
\{{\mathcal{P}}_{LL},{\mathcal{P}}_{LR},{\mathcal{P}}_{RL},{\mathcal{P}}_{RR}\}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{LL}^{\mathrm{good}}(\vec{L},\vec{R})

\displaystyle=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}}_{LL}(\vec{L},\vec{R})\ |\ p_{\mathsf{start}}=(\mathfrak{l}_{1},x_{1},y_{1}),p_{\mathsf{end}}=(\mathfrak{l}_{3},x_{2},y_{2}),y^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}_{1}=x_{2}^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{LR}^{\mathrm{good}}(\vec{L},\vec{R})

\displaystyle=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}}_{LR}(\vec{L},\vec{R})\ |\ p_{\mathsf{start}}=(\mathfrak{l}_{1},x_{1},y_{1}),p_{\mathsf{end}}=(\mathfrak{r}_{1},x_{2},y_{2}),y^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}_{1}=x_{2}^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{RL}^{\mathrm{good}}(\vec{L},\vec{R})

\displaystyle=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}}_{RL}(\vec{L},\vec{R})\ |\ p_{\mathsf{start}}=(\mathfrak{r}_{3},x_{1},y_{1}),p_{\mathsf{end}}=(\mathfrak{l}_{3},x_{2},y_{2}),y^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}_{1}=x_{2}^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}\}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{P}}_{RR}^{\mathrm{good}}(\vec{L},\vec{R})

\displaystyle=\{p\in{\mathcal{P}}_{RR}(\vec{L},\vec{R})\ |\ p_{\mathsf{start}}=(\mathfrak{r}_{3},x_{1},y_{1}),p_{\mathsf{end}}=(\mathfrak{r}_{1},x_{2},y_{2}),y^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}_{1}=x_{2}^{\mathfrak{l}(n)}\}\,.

Next we can define a graph as good as graphs which are made of good line graphs.

Definition 6.3

(Good graphs)
.

Given
L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3}
, we say
G(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
is "good" if:

1.

G(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
is a linear forest.

2.

All lines in
G(L_{1},L_{2},L_{3},R_{1},R_{2},R_{3})
are either
{\mathcal{P}}_{LL}^{\mathrm{good}}
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{LR}^{\mathrm{good}}
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{RL}^{\mathrm{good}}
or
{\mathcal{P}}_{RR}^{\mathrm{good}}
.

6.3
Parametrizing Good Graphs

Now that we have a highly structured graph associated to the database, we try to define some notation to look at these graphs. We start by defining a parametrized form of good line graphs. We know that in good line graphs, part of the output label at any vertex is repeated in the adjacent vertex. We label these by
r_{i}
. We also know that the first and the last vector also share part of the label, we denote this with
z
. The other labels are made by
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
along with
w_{1},w_{2}
. We refer the reader to figures in the
Section

2.3
. We formalise this representation below.

Definition 6.4

(Good line graph parametrization)
.

Let
p
be a line in
{\mathcal{P}}_{LL}^{\mathrm{good}}(\vec{L},\vec{R})
for some collection
\vec{L}
and
\vec{R}
, then we can write the line
p
as follows:

p=\{(\mathfrak{l}_{1},x_{0}||w_{1},z||r_{1}),(\mathfrak{l}_{2},r_{1}||x_{1},r_{2}||y_{1}),(\mathfrak{r}_{2},r_{2}||x_{2},r_{3}||y_{2}),\ldots,(\mathfrak{l}_{2},r_{n}||x_{n},r_{n+1}||y_{n}),(\mathfrak{l}_{3},z||r_{n+1},y_{0}||w_{2})\}\,.

Then we define the function

\mathfrak{p}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},\vec{x},\vec{y},w_{1},w_{2},\vec{r},z)=p\,,

where
\vec{x}
,
\vec{y}
are
\mathsf{len}(p)
-length vectors of
n-\lambda
bit strings, and
\vec{r}
is a
\mathsf{len}(p)
-length vector of
\lambda
bit strings.

We similarly define the functions
\mathfrak{p}
with the first index
{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}
,
{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}
for paths in
{\mathcal{P}}_{LR}^{\mathrm{good}}(\vec{L},\vec{R})
,
{\mathcal{P}}_{RL}^{\mathrm{good}}(\vec{L},\vec{R})
and
{\mathcal{P}}_{RR}^{\mathrm{good}}(\vec{L},\vec{R})
, respectively.

We extend the above formalization to a "good" graph (i.e. a disjoint union of good line graphs). We define the following parametrized representations of "good" graphs:

Definition 6.5

(Good graph parametrization)
.

Given any "good" graph
G
, we define the following representation: Let

G=\bigcup_{X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}}\left(\bigcup_{i}p^{XY}_{i}\right)

where
p_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}=\mathfrak{p}({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}},\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}},w^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}_{1},w^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}_{2},\overrightarrow{r^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}},z^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}),
and similarly
p_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}
,
p_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}
and
p_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}
. Then we define
\overline{{\mathcal{G}}}
as:

\displaystyle G=\overline{{\mathcal{G}}}\left(\bigcup_{X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}}\left(\bigcup_{i}\{(XY,\overrightarrow{x^{XY,i}},\overrightarrow{y^{XY,i}},w^{XY,i}_{1},w^{XY,i}_{2},\overrightarrow{r^{XY,i}},z^{XY,i})\}\right)\right).

6.4
Defining Good Auxiliary States

Next we associate even more structure with the database state. In particular, we notice that the internal labels for each line graph (i.e. the
\overrightarrow{r}
’s and
\overrightarrow{z}
’s) exist as maximally entangled pairs, with one half in the output labels with respect to some operators and the other half in the input labels with respect to some other operator. These maximally entangled pairs can be sampled later and added into the database state. Formally, given some partial description of line graphs (i.e. all parameters except the
\overrightarrow{r}
’s and
\overrightarrow{z}
’s), we can sample the rest of the parameters and create a superposition over these. We will call these partial descriptions of line graphs a "state structure parameter".

We start by defining some notation to help us formalize the above intuition. We define "state structure parameter" notation as the following four sets:

•

S_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}\}_{i}
, where
›_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}=({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}},\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}},w^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}_{1},w^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}_{2})
and
|\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}}|=|\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}}|
.

•

S_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}\}_{i}
, where
›_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}=({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}},\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}},i}},\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}},i}},w^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}},i}_{1},w^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}},i}_{2})
and
|\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}},i}}|=|\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}},i}}|
.

•

S_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}\}_{i}
, where
›_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}=({\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}},\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}},i}},\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}},i}},w^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}},i}_{1},w^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}},i}_{2})
and
|\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}},i}}|=|\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}},i}}|
.

•

S_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}\}_{i}
, where
›_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}=({\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}},\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}},i}},\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}},i}},w^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}},i}_{1},w^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}},i}_{2})
and
|\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}},i}}|=|\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}},i}}|
.

Before defining the notation to study "good states", we define the following helper functions for "state structure parameter":

Definition 6.6
.

Given a state structure parameter
\overline{S}
, with
\overline{S}=\bigcup_{X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}}S_{XY}
and
S_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}\}_{i}
,
S_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}\}_{i}
,
S_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}\}_{i}
and
S_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}\}_{i}
. Define:

•

For
X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}
,
\mathsf{len}(S_{XY})=\sum_{i\in|S_{XY}|}\mathsf{len}(\overrightarrow{x^{XY,i}})
and
\mathsf{len}(\overline{S})=\sum_{X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}}\mathsf{len}(S_{XY})
.

•

For
X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}
,
\mathsf{count}(S_{XY})=|S_{XY}|
and
\mathsf{count}(\overline{S})=\sum_{X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}}\mathsf{count}(S_{XY})
.

•

\imaginary(\overline{S})=\{w_{2}^{XY,i}|X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\},i\}
.

Before defining "Good States", we first impose an extra condition on state structure parameters.

Definition 6.7

(Good State Structure Parameter)
.

Given a state parameter
\overline{S}
, with
\overline{S}=\overline{S}=\bigcup_{X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}}S_{XY}
and
S_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}\}_{i}
,
S_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}\}_{i}
,
S_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}\}_{i}
and
S_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}=\{›_{i}^{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}\}_{i}
. We say
\overline{S}
is a good state parameter if
|\imaginary(\overline{S})|=\mathsf{count}(\overline{S})
.

Finally, we define "good states":

Definition 6.8

(Good States)
.

Given
\overline{S}=\bigcup_{X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}}S_{XY}
with
S_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}}}=\{({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},\overrightarrow{x^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}},\overrightarrow{y^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}},w^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}_{1},w^{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},i}_{2})\}_{i}
and similarly
S_{{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}}
,
S_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}}
and
S_{{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}}}
with
a=\mathsf{count}(\overline{S})
and
b=\mathsf{len}(\overline{S})
. Let, for
X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}
,
z^{XY,i}\in\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}
and
\{\overrightarrow{r^{XY,i}}\}_{X,Y,i}\in\{0,1\}^{bn}_{\mathrm{dist}}
. Say
Z=\{z^{XY,i}\}_{X,Y,i}
and
R=\{\overrightarrow{r^{XY,i}}\}_{X,Y,i}
. Define
\mathbb{G}
as the following notation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(\overline{S},R,Z)=

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{G}}}\bigg(\bigcup_{X,Y\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}}\{(XY,\overrightarrow{x^{XY,i}},\overrightarrow{y^{XY,i}},w^{XY,i}_{1},w^{XY,i}_{2},\overrightarrow{r^{XY,i}},z^{XY,i})\}_{i}\bigg)

Then we define the following state (defining notation
\mathfrak{G}
):

\displaystyle\lvert\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S})\rangle_{\mathsf{\overline{ST}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{an}(2^{\lambda})\ldots(2^{\lambda}-b+1)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Z\in\{0,1\}^{an}\\
R\in\left(\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}\right)^{b}_{\mathrm{dist}}\end{subarray}}\lvert\mathbb{G}\left(\overline{S},R,Z\right)\rangle_{\mathsf{\overline{ST}}},

where
\lvert\mathbb{G}\left(\overline{S},R,Z\right)\rangle_{\mathsf{\overline{ST}}}
denotes the
\lvert L_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\lvert L_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{2}}\lvert L_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{S}_{3}}\lvert R_{1}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{1}}\lvert R_{2}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{2}}\lvert R_{3}\rangle_{\mathsf{T}_{3}}
corresponding to the
\mathbb{G}\left(\overline{S},R,Z\right)
.

Next, we define the projector on these good state as below:

Definition 6.9

(Good Projector)
.

Define "good" projector as follows:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{S}\\
\overline{S}\text{ is good}\end{subarray}}\lvert\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S})\rangle\!\langle\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S})\rvert

6.5
Subspaces of
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}

Now that we have defined this projector
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}
, we want to say that any algorithm querying
V^{\mathsf{glued}}
, the purification mostly lies in the subspace defined by
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}
. Before we approach this, know from
Section

5.2
,
V^{\mathsf{glued}}
has different operations on different subspaces. We show how these subspaces behave with
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}
. In particular, we know that
V^{\mathsf{glued}}
has different operations on subspaces defined by projectors
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}
,
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}
and
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}
. In particular, we show that
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}
,
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}
and
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}
commute with
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}
.

From
Lemma

3.8
, we know that two projectors commute if and only if their product is a projector. Hence, to show that
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}
,
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}
and
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}
commute with
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}
, we show that their product is a projector. To define these projectors, we first define the following vectors:

•

Let
\overline{S^{\prime}}
be a good state parameter, let
X\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\overrightarrow{x}\in\{0,1\}^{(t+1)n}
and
\overrightarrow{y}\in\{0,1\}^{tn}
and
w_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}
, let
a=\mathsf{count}(\overline{S^{\prime}})
, define

\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},1}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}(2^{\lambda}-a+1)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
w^{\prime}_{2}\in\left(\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}\setminus\imaginary(\overline{S^{\prime}})\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert y_{0}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime},\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S^{\prime}}\cup\{(X{\mathcal{R}},\overrightarrow{x},y^{\prime}_{0}||\overrightarrow{y},w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2})\})\rangle_{\mathsf{AB\overline{ST}}}.

•

Let
\overline{S^{\prime}}
be a good state parameter, let
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
X\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}
,
\overrightarrow{x}\in\{0,1\}^{(t+2)n}
and
\overrightarrow{y}\in\{0,1\}^{tn}
and
w\in\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}
, let
a=\mathsf{count}(\overline{S^{\prime}})
, define

\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},2}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2^{n}\sqrt{(2^{\lambda}-a+1)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{0}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
y_{1}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
w_{2}^{\prime}\in\left(\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}\setminus\imaginary(\overline{S^{\prime}})\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert y_{0}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S^{\prime}}\cup\{(X{\mathcal{R}},\overrightarrow{x},y_{0}^{\prime}||\overrightarrow{y}||y_{1}^{\prime},w_{1},w_{2}^{\prime})\})\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC\overline{ST}}}.

•

Let
\overline{S^{\prime}}
be a good state parameter, let
\overrightarrow{x}\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
and
w\in\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}
, let
a=\mathsf{count}(\overline{S^{\prime}})
, define

\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},3}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},\overrightarrow{x},w_{1}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2^{n}\sqrt{(2^{\lambda}-a+1)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{0}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
y_{1}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
w_{2}^{\prime}\in\left(\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}\setminus\imaginary(\overline{S^{\prime}})\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert y_{0}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S^{\prime}}\cup\{({\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{R}},\overrightarrow{x},(y_{0}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime}),w_{1},w_{2}^{\prime})\})\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC\overline{ST}}}.

Notice that the above states defined are norm
1
and orthogonal.

The way to think about these states is the following:

•

The space spanned by
\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},1}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle
are the "Good" states in the image of
V^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}
(i.e. in
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}
).

•

The space spanned by
\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},2}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle
are the "Good" states in the image of
V^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}V^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}
(i.e. in
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}
).

•

The space spanned by
\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},3}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},\overrightarrow{x},w_{1}}\rangle
are the "Good" states in the image of
V^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}V^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}V^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}
(i.e. in
\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}
).

Similar to above, we also define
\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{r},i}\rangle
as:

•

Let
\overline{S^{\prime}}
be a good state parameter, let
X\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\overrightarrow{x}\in\{0,1\}^{(t+1)n}
and
\overrightarrow{y}\in\{0,1\}^{tn}
and
w_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}
, let
a=\mathsf{count}(\overline{S^{\prime}})
, define

\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{r},1}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2^{n}(2^{\lambda}-a+1)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
w^{\prime}_{2}\in\left(\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}\setminus\imaginary(\overline{S^{\prime}})\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert y_{0}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime},\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S^{\prime}}\cup\{(X{\mathcal{L}},\overrightarrow{x},y^{\prime}_{0}||\overrightarrow{y},w_{1},w^{\prime}_{2})\})\rangle_{\mathsf{AB\overline{ST}}}.

•

Let
\overline{S^{\prime}}
be a good state parameter, let
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
X\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}
,
\overrightarrow{x}\in\{0,1\}^{(t+2)n}
and
\overrightarrow{y}\in\{0,1\}^{tn}
and
w\in\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}
, let
a=\mathsf{count}(\overline{S^{\prime}})
, define

\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{r},2}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2^{n}\sqrt{(2^{\lambda}-a+1)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{0}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
y_{1}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
w_{2}^{\prime}\in\left(\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}\setminus\imaginary(\overline{S^{\prime}})\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert y_{0}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S^{\prime}}\cup\{(X{\mathcal{L}},\overrightarrow{x},y_{0}^{\prime}||\overrightarrow{y}||y_{1}^{\prime},w_{1},w_{2}^{\prime})\})\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC\overline{ST}}}.

•

Let
\overline{S^{\prime}}
be a good state parameter, let
\overrightarrow{x}\in\{0,1\}^{2n}
and
w\in\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}
, let
a=\mathsf{count}(\overline{S^{\prime}})
, define

\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{r},3}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},\overrightarrow{x},w_{1}}\rangle=\frac{1}{2^{n}\sqrt{(2^{\lambda}-a+1)}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y_{0}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
y_{1}^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}^{n}\\
w_{2}^{\prime}\in\left(\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}\setminus\imaginary(\overline{S^{\prime}})\right)\end{subarray}}\lvert y_{0}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime},\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S^{\prime}}\cup\{({\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{L}},\overrightarrow{x},(y_{0}^{\prime},y_{1}^{\prime}),w_{1},w_{2}^{\prime})\})\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC\overline{ST}}}.

Finally, we have the following lemmas that formalize that
\lvert\chi^{i}\rangle
’s span
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},i}
:

Lemma 6.10
.

We have the following:

\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}=\sum_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},1}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle\!\langle\chi^{\mathfrak{l},1}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rvert

Lemma 6.11
.

We have the following:

\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}=\sum_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},2}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle\!\langle\chi^{\mathfrak{l},2}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rvert

Lemma 6.12
.

We have the following:

\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}=\sum_{\overline{S^{\prime}},\overrightarrow{x},w_{1}}\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},3}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},\overrightarrow{x},w_{1}}\rangle\!\langle\chi^{\mathfrak{l},3}_{\overline{S^{\prime}},\overrightarrow{x},w_{1}}\rvert

Similar to above, we have
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},i}
as a projector on space spanned by
\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{r},i}\rangle
. We give proofs for the above lemmas in
Appendix

C
.

6.6
Action of
W^{\mathsf{glued}}
on states in
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}

Now finally we can start talking about the action of
W^{\mathsf{glued}}
on states in
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}
. In particular, we want to show that querying
W^{\mathsf{glued}}
on a state in
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}
gives us states in
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}
. Recall that:

\displaystyle W^{\mathsf{glued}}=

\displaystyle\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},321}\right)\cdot V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{L}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{L}^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot\left(I-\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},32}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},321}\right)\cdot V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{L}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},1}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},3}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},32}\right)\cdot V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}\cdot V_{R}^{(2),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},12}-\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(I-\Pi^{{\mathcal{L}},3}\right)\cdot V_{R}^{(3),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot V_{R}^{(2),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\cdot\left(\Pi^{{\mathcal{R}},123}\right)

Recall that from the previous section, we know exactly how to characterize good states in the four branches. In particular, we know that these states in various branches are spanned by
\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},i}\rangle
. We show the action of
W^{\mathsf{glued}}
on
\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},i}\rangle
. In particular, we show the following:

Lemma 6.13
.

Let
\overline{S}
be some good state parameter and
x_{0},x_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
,
w_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}
. Then we have the following:

\displaystyle V^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}V^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}V^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}_{R}\lvert x_{0}\rangle\lvert w_{1}\rangle\lvert x_{1}\rangle\lvert\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S})\rangle

\displaystyle=\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},3}_{\overline{S},(x_{0},x_{1}),w_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle V^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}_{L}V^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}_{L}V^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}_{L}\lvert x_{0}\rangle\lvert w_{1}\rangle\lvert x_{1}\rangle\lvert\mathfrak{G}(\overline{S})\rangle

\displaystyle=\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{r},3}_{\overline{S},(x_{0},x_{1}),w_{1}}\rangle

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
\overline{S^{\prime}}
be a good state parameter, let
X\in\{{\mathcal{L}},{\mathcal{R}}\}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
,
\overrightarrow{x}\in\{0,1\}^{(t+1)n}
and
\overrightarrow{y}\in\{0,1\}^{tn}
,
x_{0}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
w_{1}\in\{0,1\}^{{\lambda}}
. Then we have the following:

\displaystyle V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid}}V_{R}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{r},1}_{\overline{S},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle_{\mathsf{AB\overline{ST}}}\lvert x^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle=\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},2}_{\overline{S},X,\overrightarrow{x}||x^{\prime},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC\overline{ST}}}

\displaystyle V_{L}^{(3),\mathsf{mid}}V_{L}^{(2),\mathsf{mid}}V_{R}^{(1),\mathsf{mid},\dagger}\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{l},1}_{\overline{S},X,\overrightarrow{x},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle_{\mathsf{AB\overline{ST}}}\lvert x^{\prime}\rangle_{\mathsf{C}}

\displaystyle=\lvert\chi^{\mathfrak{r},2}_{\overline{S},X,\overrightarrow{x}||x^{\prime},\overrightarrow{y},w_{1}}\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC\overline{ST}}}

We provide the proofs of the above lemmas in
Appendix

D
. Finally, now since we understand the action of
W^{\mathsf{glued}}
on basis states of the subspaces, we show that applying
W^{\mathsf{glued}}
on good states gives good states. Formally, we show the following lemma:

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\lvert\phi\rangle
be some state such that
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}\lvert\phi\rangle=\lvert\phi\rangle
, then
(I-\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}})W^{\mathsf{glued}}\lvert\phi\rangle=0
.

The proof of the above lemma is in
Appendix

D
. Symmetrically, we can also get the lemma below.

Lemma 6.16
.

Let
\lvert\phi\rangle
be some state such that
\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}\lvert\phi\rangle=\lvert\phi\rangle
, then
(I-\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}})W^{\mathsf{glued},\dagger}\lvert\phi\rangle=0
.

6.7
Purification of
W^{\mathsf{glued}}
is Good

Let
{\cal A}
denote a
2t
-query algorithm. For any unitaries
U
, define

\lvert{\cal A}^{U,U^{\dagger}}\rangle=\prod_{i=1}^{t}\left(U^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{ABC}}B_{i}U_{\mathsf{ABC}}A_{i}\right)\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{ABC}}\lvert 0\rangle_{\mathsf{D}}.

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
{\cal A}
denote a
2t
-query algorithm. Define

\displaystyle\lvert\psi\rangle

\displaystyle=\lvert{\cal A}^{W^{\mathsf{glued}},W^{\mathsf{glued},\dagger}}\rangle

\displaystyle\lvert\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\lvert{\cal A}^{\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}W^{\mathsf{glued}},\Pi^{\mathsf{Good}}W^{\mathsf{glued},\dagger}}\rangle

Then

\lvert\psi\rangle=\lvert\phi\rangle

We get the above by
Lemmas

6.15
and
6.16
.

7
Strong Gluing Theorem

In this section, we finally prove the Strong Gluing Theorem. We start by reducing the large Haar Unitary to the modified path recording and the glued construction to the modified glued path recording. Next we define an operator that simulates the purification in the large path recording from the purification in the gluing construction. To do this, we do a query-by-query analysis.

We now state the main result of this section.

Theorem 7.1

(Strong gluing of random unitaries)
.

Let
\mathsf{A},\mathsf{B},\mathsf{C}
be registers, and
U^{1}_{\mathsf{AB}}
,
U^{2}_{\mathsf{BC}}
, and
U^{3}_{\mathsf{AB}}
be Haar random unitaries on
n+{\lambda}
qubits, with
\mathsf{B}
being
{\lambda}
qubits. Then for any
t
-query adversary
\mathcal{A}^{(\cdot)}
, the following holds

\mathsf{TD}\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{U^{1},U^{2},U^{3}\leftarrow\mu_{n}}\left[\lvert\mathcal{A}^{U^{3}_{\mathsf{AB}}U^{2}_{\mathsf{BC}}U^{1}_{\mathsf{AB}},(U^{3}_{\mathsf{AB}}U^{2}_{\mathsf{BC}}U^{1}_{\mathsf{AB}})^{\dagger}}\rangle\!\langle\mathcal{A}^{U^{3}_{\mathsf{AB}}U^{2}_{\mathsf{BC}}U^{1}_{\mathsf{AB}},(U^{3}_{\mathsf{AB}}U^{2}_{\mathsf{BC}}U^{1}_{\mathsf{AB}})^{\dagger}}\rvert\right],\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{O\leftarrow\mu_{2n-\lambda}}\left[\lvert\mathcal{A}^{O_{\mathsf{ABC}},O^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{ABC}}}\rangle\!\langle\mathcal{A}^{O_{\mathsf{ABC}},O^{\dagger}_{\mathsf{ABC}}}\rvert\right]\right)\\
=O\left(\frac{t^{2}}{2^{{\lambda}/2}}+\frac{t^{3}}{2^{{\lambda}}}+\frac{t^{3}}{2^{(n+{\lambda})/8}}\right).
